ParlaMint-PL_2021-07-21-sejm-35-1.u423	Mrs. Marshal! High Seym! On July 28th, on the birthday of Baruch Samuel Blumberg, who identified the hepatitis B virus, we will celebrate the World Day of Hepatitis B. Professor Baruch Samuel Blumberg was born on 28 July 1925 and died on 5 April 2011. He's an American physician, an astrobiologist, Nobel Prize laureate in Physiology and Medicine for identifying the hepatitis B virus. World Hepatitis Day was established by the European Liver Patients' Association in 2004 on 19 May 2010 by the WHO, the United Nations World Health Organisation, which is responsible for health protection, formally recognised World Hepatitis Day. The organization appointed for the 28th of July. Hepatitis is the fourth disease recognised by WHO - alongside malaria, tuberculosis and HIV - where the organisation is taking action to raise public health awareness. It is estimated that almost 500 million people worldwide are affected by hepatitis B and C. Many of them do not realize that they have a virus in them, which poses a serious risk, because the consequence of not taking treatment may be cirrhosis of the liver and death. The main objective of establishing a global day of hepatitis is to raise public awareness of hepatitis and to encourage participation in preventive research. Hepatitis is a virus-induced disease that is transmitted through blood, sexual contact, unsterilised equipment. Infections can occur through various types of treatments, the use of infected toothbrushes, razors, in the tattoo studio. I'm gonna call in some data. The following types of hepatitis A are distinguished: Hepatitis A, which occurs mainly through the gastrointestinal tract. It does not cause cancer. Type-B and type-D inflammations for which infection occurs as a result of contact with infected blood, sexual contact. They can lead to very serious hepatitis and liver failure, and then to death. Hepatitis C, where infection occurs by contact with infected blood, sexual contact, unsterilised equipment. The treatment is symptomatic. HCV may destroy the liver for many years without clear symptoms. Continuous fatigue and flu-like symptoms are just some fairly unexpected signs that may indicate that we are infected and that we are suffering from hepatitis C. Hepatitis E primarily concerns Asia. The virus isn't dangerous. Let's go back to World Hepatitis Day. Each year the event takes place under a different slogan. This day is celebrated mainly in order to increase knowledge and to show how to avoid infection with the virus. It is worth to participate on this day in happenings, lectures that are organized in our country. Despite the ongoing pandemic in Poland, anyone without exception can quickly without records, free of charge, perform a test for hepatitis C, i.e. for the presence of HCV virus. The list of all points with the exact addresses is provided on hcv.info. en. Download points are located throughout the country and can be checked for HCV infection free of charge. In case of a positive anti-CV test, the patient is guaranteed to continue free diagnostics, viral RNA tests, HCV antibodies. Today, Polish patients have access to modern treatment, thanks to which they can get rid of the virus from the blood already in a few weeks. All the more so, I encourage you to take advantage of available free research. Let's all join in the celebration of the World Day of Hepatitis. It's July 28th. Thank you. [[Applause]]

ParlaMint-PL_2021-07-21-sejm-35-1.u437	Mrs. Marshal! High Izbo! Ladies and gentlemen! Premiere - a city that smells like history. A while ago, I introduced the Zels in my speech. Today I want to talk about another city, a village located south of Piotrków Trybunalski, on Pilica, on the historical border between the former districts: Sieradzka and Sandomierz. The pre-election, as it is mentioned, lies away from motorways and other major transport routes. Life today flows slowly, with its rhythm, but once upon a time, this small town became the epicenter of important events, a place where history simply happens. It is unknown where the name Przebórz came from, its toponomastics are unclear. Perhaps it comes from the popular name Predbor in the Middle Ages, which may have been the founder of the settlement. Another version assumes that it was its location in front of the powerful forest complexes, the bords named Predborz. The first mention of the village dates back to the 12th century, and it became a town in 1370. He liked them the last of the Piasts on the Polish throne - Kazimierz Wielki, who as a great hunter was in this area very often and just somewhere he made his last hunt, during which he broke his leg, which consequently led to his death. It is in Predborz, as few know, that King Władysław Jagiełło granted in July 1423 the town rights of the village called Lodz, which is today just Łódź. The successful development of the city stopped the Swedish Deluge. Many disasters and conflagrations have passed through them. It divided the fate of the Republic of Poland. In the 19th century, the famous textile industry was located in Preborz. The company Jan Lange - John Cockerill was created here. Steam machines were brought in from Belgium, foreign professionals were hired and dress production started. Many residents took part in the January Uprising, and several skirmishes near the town were fought by a party of the famous guerrilla Józef Oxinski. After regaining independence, the city was a very important local shopping centre. People of many cultures and religions lived side by side in the town. The Jewish community was particularly large. World War II did not deal kindly with the Pre-Governor. The Germans destroyed part of the market, the synagogue. They organized a forced labor camp in the city. The city lost more than half of its population. The Germans deliberately murdered all pre-Borbon Jews. The smell of the famous pre-Borbon roast cougle on every Sabbath Friday has gone forever into the past. After 1945, everyday life came to the town, but war wounds continued to be known for a long time. In 1958, the people of Preborza fought for their faith. When the communist authorities, using their vacation, took down the crosses present in the school halls, parents protested loudly, trying to change this state. Many civil militia and security officers were used to pacify the spontaneous protest. The people most involved in the protest were punished by imprisonment, detention, fine, expulsion from work. ParlaMint-PL_2021-07-21-sejm-35-1.u447	Mrs. Marshal! High Seym! On March 24, we celebrated a national day of memory of Poles saving Jews throughout the country. This feast was established to honor and honor thousands of Polish families who saved many lives during the German occupation. These are the stories of those families who hid the Jews also under their own roof. Many times our countrymen sacrificed their lives to save others. I would like to present the story of one of the families in my district who were helping the Jews back then. June 18th this year An plaque commemorating this heroic family was unveiled in Gruszów by Mr Kazimierz Łapczyński. Aniela and Franciszek Szewczyk, together with three children, lived in the village of Gruszów in the municipality of Raciechowice in the district of Thoughtnicki in a small house. During World War I, Franciszek Szewczyk was incorporated into the Austrian army and later also participated in the Polish-Bolshevik war in which he was wounded. In the autumn of 1941, the Jewish Goldberg family, Jerzy and Giza, and their two children, Lilka and Jan, asked for their help. The Shewczy family was aware of the penalties of the Germans, yet she decided to help those in need. The Jewish family was kept and fed for almost a year in a specially warm granary next to the house of the Shewczyk family. When the Goldbergs managed to get fake IDs, they left for Bochnia. However, their moving did not end the action of helping Jews by a brave family from Gruszow. Soon after the departure of the Goldbergs, the Shewczyks hid three people from another Jewish family - the Federgrün. All of this family survived the war, while Jerzy Goldberg died in Bochnia with a natural death, and the Jewish family moved to Cracow, where she also found safe shelter. The son of Jan Goldberg-Kruczkowski under the pseudonym "Domeyko" became a soldier of the Home Army, died in the fight against the Germans on 22 October 1944. My mother and my daughter were lucky to see the end of the war, and they both left for France. After the war, the Szewicz family also hid guerrillas, for which she suffered many repressions by Communists. Mary's daughter was sentenced to 3.5 years in prison, Joseph's son, for 8 months. But Francis and Angela Shewczyk were also in prison. The youngest daughter of the Goldberg family applied for the title of the Righteous Among the World Nations, and in the same year the title was granted to them. Maria from the house of Federgrün and her sister also gave a witness to the aid received from the family of Shewczyk after the war. I draw attention to this story, because it shows the great heroism and sacrifice that led this family, as well as many others when saving Jewish citizens. It is our duty to cultivate the memory of such people. As an MP, I recalled the history of my family from my territory, but there are many more stories and testimonies. As a parliamentarian, I feel obliged to commemorate the heroes of that time. It is important to keep mentioning them and to talk about them also here, on the Parliament's platform. Our strength is the memory of the past. That's where we should build the future. We are also here to pass this memory on to the younger generation of Poles. We must never forget our history. A nation without the past, without tradition and without culture is not respected by anyone. Hello and praise the heroes! [[Applause]]

ParlaMint-PL_2021-07-21-sejm-35-1.u464	On July 7, 1944, at dawn, the troops of the National Army hit German positions around Vilnius, inside the city the troops of the Vilnius Garrison of the National Army began to join the fight. The Vilnius Uprising, known as Operation Ostra Gate, began. On the same day, Red Army troops approached the city from the east, also starting a fight for Vilnius. Poles themselves liberated the right bank of the city, followed by crossing the Wilia River and fighting together with the Soviets in the centre of Vilnius, moving towards the Sharp Gate. Vilnius was liberated on 13 July 1944. On the same day at Krawczuna, the AK troops fought the last battle with the German crew of Vilnius, which was retreating to the west. Several hundred soldiers of the National Army died in the fight for Vilnius, witnessing their patriotism and attachment to the Kresów of the Republic. Respect and memory fighting for free Poland.

ParlaMint-PL_2021-07-21-sejm-35-1.u466	Mr. Marshal! High Seym! On 9 December 2019 the European Commission presented the assumptions of the European Green Deal. Europe is expected to become climate neutral by 2050. The European Commission is planning changes in many areas: energy, transport, agriculture, steel, cement, ICT, textiles and chemicals. However, there is no shortage of criticism of the objectives of this programme. According to the realists, it is too radical, and the targets are threatening both the business and the economies of the Member States, mainly Poland, whose energy is based in more than 70% on coal. It should be added that, thanks to the massive support of the United Rights Government, the share of RES increased to 26% in May 2021. As a country with a large share of coal in energy for many years, we have consistently stressed that climate change must be fair and must not worsen economic conditions in specific regions or social groups. Fit for 55 lacks recognition of the role of gas, whose sources of supply our government diversifys as transitional fuel in the funds proposed by the EC. For Poland, this is a key transitional fuel which is an essential part of a fair energy transformation. Poland does not see the possibility of implementing the target without recognising the role of gas at this transitional stage. For these reasons, Poland will remain an active partner for the EC and other Member States. The EU also increasingly announces the introduction of a carbon tax that would be levied on fuels in proportion to the greenhouse gas emissions generated by their combustion. The European Commission calls for Member States to give up coal in a short time and move towards renewable energy sources. In May 2020, MEPs from the European Conservatives and Reformists, including MEPs, called on the Presidents of the European Council, the European Commission and the European Parliament to postpone legislative initiatives on Green Governance, as the EU must now be a priority to get out of the crisis caused by the coravirus. The climate minister has repeatedly expressed the view that the solutions to the Green Deal before the pandemic did not take into account the economic crisis. The Minister expects its implementation to be slowed down, especially in the agricultural sector. The sceptics on the Green Deal do not really think that the proposals for directives and regulations are being made in the European Commission, but these directives and regulations are dictated by lobbyists. Polish energy and the Polish economy are hit by ETS prices, which have now been speculatively elevated over a period of several months from EUR 25 to EUR 60 per tonne. This causes huge costs for CO2 emissions in coal-based power plants. Western countries, which do not have historical experiences such as Poland and have dominant nuclear energy in the mix, are in favour of accelerating the EU's climate policy. There is a real threat that the aspiring companies in our region of Europe will be choked by climate legislation and subjected to unequal confrontation with Western competition. If the European Commission fails to submit an economic impact assessment, the entire Fit for 55 plan may be annulled by some EU leaders at the European Council. It is unofficially known that the veto does not exclude Viktor Orbán, Hungarian Prime Minister. Poland generally supports the achievement of climate objectives and realises that it is necessary to transform the Polish economy towards a low-carbon one, because it will cost more than necessary changes. Successive, but not radically modernising the national energy system and reducing its carbon footprint is the best way to ensure the competitiveness of our economy and to mitigate the consequences of climate policy on energy prices. Our government is aware that lower emissions mean lower fees for the power generation sector. Therefore, Poland is already introducing new programmes at national level, such as clean air, my current and other investment programmes - e.g. in the field of RES and energy efficiency - which brings us closer to the objectives pursued.
